The (3,4, ∞) modular family of 2-tori, completed at its three special points, is a
complex structure on S6.
The base and the family. Let ∆ = ∆(3,4, ∞) be the triangle group, acting on the upper half plane h; the
quotient h/ ∆ is P1 minus a point, and adding the cusp gives P1 with orbifold points of orders 3 and 4 and the
cusp. Let V ∼ = Z4 carry automorphisms T ,T of orders 3 and 4 with T := (T T )−1 unipotent, (T −I)2 = 0:
1 2 0 1 2 0
thisisarepresentation ∆ → SL(V), andweletitactontheduallattice Λ = V∗ by A = (T −1)t (§2). Theperiod
j j
functions τ,µ,β on h are an equivariant period map for it (§3), and the quotient is a family of complex 2-tori
over the punctured orbifold curve: the analogue of a universal family of abelian surfaces over a modular curve,
with this rank-four lattice representation in place of the symplectic one. The very general fibre is not algebraic.
The very general fibre F has NS(F) = Z η with η a single indefinite class, and the algebraic dimension of the
completed threefold is a(X) = 1 (§9).
The three special fibres. The three conjugacy classes of maximal finite and parabolic cyclic subgroups of ∆
single out the three special points, and at these three points we take the three standard completions; each is a
choice, and every ℓ and every admissible v below occurs. At an elliptic point of order m the local monodromy
0 j
is a torus automorphism of order m, and the filling is Kodaira’s logarithmic transform: (disc×torus)/(Z /m),
the generator acting by a rotation of the disc and by the automorphism followed by a translation; the reduced
fibre is of bielliptic type (§5). At the cusp the monodromy is unipotent of rank two, and the filling N is
0
Mumford’s toric degeneration; its central fibre W is read off the prescribed A -triangulation of R2, the deck
2
action being the one in which the unimodular map B : Λ¯ → Λ induced by M −I = (T −1)t−I on Λ enters.
0 tor 0 0
Modulo the lattice the triangulation has one vertex, three edges and two triangles, i.e. W is one toric surface
with hexagonal moment polygon — the degree-six del Pezzo dP — glued to itself along opposite sides, with
6
two triple points (§4). Thus there is a single family, completed in the three standard ways at the three kinds of
special point of an orbifold curve with a cusp.
The gluing and the fundamental group. Each filling is glued to the global family along a collar by a fibre-
preserving map, and we reglue by translations by local sections (§6, Prop. 6.3); among these, π 1 and H ∗ depend
only on three integers ℓ (at the cusp), ℓ ,ℓ (at the elliptic points) (§7). Let γ ∈ V be the linear coordinate on Λ
0 1 2
whose kernel kerγ = ⟨uˆ,wˆ,δˆ⟩ is the span of all (A −1)Λ ; then the coinvariants Λ /kerγ ∼ = Z are detected by
j
γ alone, and ℓ = γ(v ) for the translation vectors v . The result is π (X) ∼ = Z /|12ℓ −4ℓ −3ℓ | (§7).1 This
j j j 1 0 1 2
formula admits a reading, which the body does not use. It is the formula |a a e +a b +a b | of [Orl72] for
1 2 0 2 1 1 2
the order of H of the Seifert fibred space over S2(a ,a ), evaluated at e = ℓ and b = −ℓ — the sign coming
1 1 2 0 0 j j

j j j 1 2

withthecircleaction(z,w) (cid:55)→ (λ3z,λ4w),whoseorbitspaceisS2(3,4)[Orl72]. Heuristicallythetwistcondition
says: glue so that the coinvariant circle of the fibres traces out the (3,4) Seifert fibration of the three-sphere
over the base.
ThehomologyandtheEulernumber. Theremainingtorusdirectionscontributenothing: wˆ andδˆarevanishing
cycles at the cusp, collapsed in the hexagonal fibre, and the rest is killed by monodromy, the coinvariants of
Λ having rank one. With the twists above, X has the integral homology of S6 and is diffeomorphic to S6 (§7,
§8). The Euler number localises at the cusp: e(X) = e(W) = 2 (§7), every other fibre being a torus or a free
torus quotient and so of Euler number 0 — one singular fibre carries the whole Euler characteristic of S6, as the
twelve nodal fibres carry the 12 of a rational elliptic surface.
Why the argument of [CDP20] does not apply. That argument requires a line bundle non-torsion on every
fibre and propagates a vanishing statement through each singular fibre by way of its normalisation. Here
H2(X; Z) = 0 and Pic(X) ∼ = C , so every line bundle is topologically trivial; at the non-normal toric fibre the
differential of the fibration itself supplies the section which the passage to the normalisation assumes away,
and R2f ∗ (T X ⊗L) ̸= 0 for all L. The count of H 1 in [CDP20, Lemma 4.2] (after [CDP98, Lemma 3.2]) assumes
trivial monodromy, whereas the monodromy representation here is non-trivial; §10 shows that this last point is
repairable for X, while the failure of the reduction at the non-normal fibre is decisive.

Theorem. X is a compact connected complex 3-manifold and f: X → B is a surjective holomorphic map with
connected fibres.
(1) Over B◦ the map f is a proper submersion whose fibres are complex 2-tori. The fibre f−1(p ) is the divisor
0
W ⊂ N , which is reduced, irreducible and has normal crossings. The normalisation η identifies opposite
0
sides of the hexagon of (−1)-curves of dP , the double locus is three rational curves through two triple
6
points, and e(W) = 2. For j = 1,2 one has f∗(p ) = m S , where S := (f−1(p )) is a smooth bielliptic
j j j j j red
surface in N, and O (S )| has order m .
j X j Sj j
(2) The threefold X is simply connected, since π 1 (X) ∼ = Z /|12ℓ 0 −4ℓ 1 −3ℓ 2 | = 1, and H ∗ (X; Z) ∼ = H ∗ (S6; Z);
consequently X is diffeomorphic to S6 (§7ff.).
(3) The algebraic dimension of X is a(X) = 1 and M(X) = f∗C(t): every meromorphic function on X is
constant along the fibres of f.
(4) The canonical bundle is K ∼ = f∗O (−1)⊗O (2S ) and is not torsion; c (X) = 2; and Aut0(X) ∼ = C∗ acts
X B X 2 3
along the fibres of f with fixed locus a double curve of W (§9).

the del Pezzo surface of degree 6, the blow up of

in three non-collinear points, whose anticanonical
cycle is a hexagon of six (−1)-curves. A bielliptic surface is a smooth compact complex surface (E×F)/G
with E,F elliptic curves and G a finite group of translations of E acting on F with F/G ∼ = P1 [BHPV, V.5],
[BdF], [Ser]. We write M(Y) for the field of meromorphic functions of a compact complex space Y, and
a(Y) = tr.deg M(Y) for its algebraic dimension.

Theorem. There exist a compact connected complex manifold X of dimension 3 and a surjective holomor-
phic map f: X → B = P1 with connected fibres, with the following properties.
(1) f isaproperholomorphicsubmersionover B◦, andeveryfibre f−1(p) with p ∈ B◦ isacomplextorus
of dimension 2.
(2) The fibreW := f−1(p ) is reduced, irreducible, and a normal crossings divisor in X: at every point of
0
W the function t ◦ f is, in suitable local coordinates, z , z z or z z z . The normalisation of W is

6 6

both triple points of W and otherwise pairwise disjoint; and e(W) = 2.
(3) The fibres over p and p are multiple: as divisors, f∗(p ) = 3S and f∗(p ) = 4S , where S =

(4) X is simply connected and H ∗ (X; Z) ∼ = H ∗ (S6; Z); in particular b 2 (X) = b 3 (X) = 0 and e(X) =
c (X) = 2 = e(W). Consequently X is diffeomorphic to the 6-sphere.
3
(5) a(X) = 1, and f is the algebraic reduction of X: M(X) = f∗M(B) = f∗C(t), that is, every
meromorphic function on X is the pull back under f of a rational function on B; moreover the very
general fibre of f has algebraic dimension 0.
(6) f ∗ O X = O B , R1f ∗ O X ∼ = O B ⊕O B (−1) and R2f ∗ O X ∼ = O B (−1); moreover
h0,1(X) = 1, h0,2(X) = h0,3(X) = 0, h1,0(X) = h2,0(X) = h3,0(X) = 0.

Corollary 1.1. The six-sphere S6 carries an integrable complex structure, namely the one obtained by
transporting the complex structure of X along a diffeomorphism X → S6.

Remark 1.3 (Hodge numbers not computed here). All the Hodge numbers of X are determined by item
(6) except h1,1 and h1,2 = h2,1. Since ∑ (−1)p+qhp,q = e(X) on any compact complex manifold (the Euler
p,q
characteristic is unchanged through the Frölicher spectral sequence), equivalently by the Hirzebruch–
Riemann–Rochidentity ∑ (−1)pχ(Ωp) = c (X) (§9.4),thevalues χ(O ) = 0, e(X) = 2andthevanishings
p X 3 X
of item (6) force h1,1 = h2,2 = h1,2 +1. Only h1,2 is left undetermined; we expect h1,2 = 1 (whence
h1,1 = h2,2 = 2). A computation would require the direct images Rqf ∗ Ω1 X and Rqf ∗ Ω2 X , hence a description
Ωp
of the sheaves along the three singular fibres, where they have torsion, together with an analysis of
X/B
the corresponding Leray spectral sequences in the light of the non-degeneration recorded in item (6).
The construction is explicit. Over B◦ one has a family J → B◦ of 2-tori whose period matrix is written
down in closed form in terms of three functions τ,µ,β on the upper half plane h uniformising the
z
orbifold (B;3,4, ∞): τ is the elliptic modular parameter, j(τ) = 1728t, while µ and β solve prescribed
inhomogeneous transformation laws. The local monodromies at p and p have orders 3 and 4, and the
1 2
localmonodromyat p isunipotentwithsquare-zerologarithm. Thethreefibresover Σ arefilledinbythree
0
local models: at p by a quotient of an infinite smooth toric threefold built on the A -triangulation of the
0 2
plane, in the spirit of Mumford’s construction of degenerating abelian varieties [Mum72, AMRT, KKMS];
at p and p by logarithmic transforms in the sense of Kodaira [Kod64], with twist vectors v = ε and
1 2 1
v = −ε′, explicit vectors of the period lattice fixed by the respective local monodromies. The three twists
2
arewhatmakes X simplyconnected: §7givesπ (X) ∼ = Z /|p|Z with p = 12ℓ −4ℓ −3ℓ ,where(ℓ ,ℓ ,ℓ )
1 0 1 2 0 1 2
records the twist data, and the above choice yields (ℓ ,ℓ ,ℓ ) = (0,1,−1), so p = −1.

1.2 Relation to [CDP20]
The Main Theorem is incompatible with a published result. Campana, Demailly and Peternell proved
in [CDP98] that a compact complex threefold X with b (X) = 0 and a(X) > 0 has c (X) = e(X) = 0,
2 3
and deduced that a compact complex threefold homeomorphic to S6 has algebraic dimension 0. That
proof rests on [CDP98, Lemma 1.5], which is incorrect as stated; the corrigendum [CDP20] says so and
re-proves the results in two cases. [CDP20, Thm. 2.1] treats the case in which there exists a non-constant
meromorphic map g: X (cid:57)(cid:57)(cid:75) P1 which is not holomorphic (this case is written out in detail in [LRS,
Thm. 3.1, Cor. 3.2], following [CDP98]), while [CDP20, Thm. 2.2] treats the case H1(X, Z) = H2(X, Z) = 0,
a(X) = 1 with holomorphic algebraic reduction f: X → C, concluding (a) Hi(X,T ⊗M) = 0 for i ̸= 1 and
X
generic M ∈ Pic0(X), (b) χ(X,T ⊗M) ≤ 0 and (c) c (X) ≤ 0; together these give [CDP20, Cor. 2.3]: if X
X 3
is homeomorphic to S6 then a(X) = 0. The manifold of the Main Theorem has H1(X, Z) = H2(X, Z) = 0,
a(X) = 1, holomorphic algebraic reduction f: X → P1, and c (X) = 2 > 0, so that conclusion (c)
3
fails; so does conclusion (b), since c (X) = c (X) = 0 and Pic(X) = Pic0(X) give, by Riemann–Roch,
1 2
χ(X,T ⊗ M) = 1c (X) = 1 > 0 for every M ∈ Pic(X). It therefore contradicts [CDP20, Thm. 2.2] and
X 2 3
[CDP20, Cor. 2.3], as well as [CDP20, Lemma 4.2] (the special case g = 0, b = 0 of [CDP98, Lemma
1
3.2]), [CDP20, Cor. 5.2] and [CDP20, Prop. 5.5] (= [CDP98, Thm. 3.1, Rem. 3.8(1)]), whose conclusions
fail for X (their printed proofs presuppose trivial monodromy); this failure is repairable for the purpose
of the proof of Cor. 2.3 at X (§10), unlike the reduction at the non-normal fibre, which is decisive. It
does not contradict [CDP20, Thm. 2.1] or [LRS], whose hypothesis is the existence of some non-constant
non-holomorphic meromorphic map X (cid:57)(cid:57)(cid:75) P1, and no such map exists on our X: by item (5) of the Main
Theorem M(X) = f∗C(t), so every non-constant meromorphic map X (cid:57)(cid:57)(cid:75) P1 is of the form h◦ f with h a
non-constant rational function on B, hence is holomorphic.
Section 10 is devoted to this conflict and locates the step at which the argument of [CDP20] and the
example part company. The key point is the fibre W = f−1(p ): it is a normal crossings divisor which
0
is not normal, the six sides of its hexagon being glued to each other in three opposite pairs (item (2) of
the Main Theorem). We prove (Theorem 10.5) that for every holomorphic line bundle L on X one has
R2f ∗ (T X ⊗L) ̸= 0, the obstruction being supported at p 0 and arising from the section df ⊗e of a sheaf
supported on W which exists precisely because W is non-normal. The vanishing of this second direct
image is the hypothesis under which [CDP20, Prop. 2.4] is invoked in the proof of [CDP20, Thm. 2.2]; for
X it fails, forevery L, and sothe proofof [CDP20, Thm.2.2] does notcover X. Section 10 alsoshows, inthe
5
=== PAGE 6 ===
light of the monodromy group of f computed in §3, that the conclusions of [CDP20, Lemma 4.2], [CDP20,
Cor. 5.2] and [CDP20, Prop. 5.5] fail for X as well. Section 10 discusses the two steps of the printed proofs
that do not apply to X; the two statements cannot both be correct. For this reason the construction is given
with every matrix, chart and identification explicit.

1.4 Context
The question whether S6 admits a complex structure goes back to Hopf [Hopf48]; for its history and the
state of the art we refer to the surveys [AGr, Ang18]. Among the even-dimensional spheres only S2 and S6
carry almost complex structures; the structure on S6 induced by octonionic multiplication is not integrable,
its Nijenhuis tensor being nowhere zero, and the space of almost complex structures inducing a given
orientation is connected, so that no homotopy-theoretic invariant can separate a hypothetical integrable
structure from the octonionic one. The one general structural theorem in this direction is that of LeBrun
[LeB87], anticipated by Blanchard: no complex structure on S6 is orthogonal with respect to the round
metric; the proof shows that such a structure would make S6 Kähler, contradicting b = 0, and the later
2
refinements also use only H2(S6) = 0. The structure constructed here is not produced from a metric and
carries no a priori relation to the round one, so [LeB87] does not bear on it.
Compact complex threefolds with b = 0 are classical: Calabi and Eckmann [CE53] put complex
2
structures on S3×S3 and on products of odd-dimensional spheres; these, the Hopf threefolds S1×S5,
the quotients SL(2, C)/ Γ and the principal elliptic bundles of [LRS, §4] furnish many examples, with
a = 0,1,2 (see [CDP98, §4]). What distinguishes S6 among these is e = 2 ̸= 0. The complex-analytic
constraints on a hypothetical complex structure on S6 have been studied in detail: Huckleberry, Kebekus
and Peternell [HKP] proved that such an X is not almost homogeneous, that h0(T ) ≤ 2, H3(X,O ) = 0,
X X
h0,1 = h0,2+1 and Pic(X) = Pic0(X) ∼ = H1(X,O ), and analysed the possible fixed-point sets of a C∗-
X
action; Lehn, Rollenske and Schinko [LRS] obtained bounds on spaces of sections, and wrote out in detail
the meromorphic-map case of [CDP98, CDP20], following [CDP98]. Constructions of complex structures
on S6 have been claimed by Etesi [Ete15] by entirely different means, from Samelson structures on G and
2
conjugate orbits; nothing here depends on them or bears on their validity.
The two mechanisms combined here are classical. Logarithmic transformations were introduced by
Kodaira for elliptic surfaces [Kod64] (see also [BHPV, V.13]), where they are the standard device for
changing the fundamental group of an elliptic fibration without changing the base; the Dolgachev surface
obtained from a rational elliptic surface by logarithmic transformations of coprime multiplicities is simply
connected [Dol81], and the present construction is an analogue one dimension up, with 2-tori in place
of elliptic curves. Filling in a degenerating family of abelian varieties by a toroidal model over the
nilpotent monodromy cone goes back to Mumford [Mum72], and in the arithmetic setting to [AMRT]; the
7
=== PAGE 8 ===
combinatorial input used here is the A -triangulation of the plane. What is specific to the present situation
2
is that the two mechanisms are used simultaneously, over a base
P1
with exactly three critical points, for a
representation ρ of the (3,4, ∞) triangle group whose local monodromies have orders 3, 4 and ∞ , and
V
that the resulting central fibre W at the unipotent point is non-normal, with e(W) = 2 accounting for the
whole Euler characteristic of X.

As on page 1 (Setup), V is free of rank 4 with ordered basis (γ,u,w,δ) and Λ := V∗ carries the dual basis
(γˆ,uˆ,wˆ,δˆ); we write ⟨λ,x⟩ := λ(x), elements are columns, matrices act on columns from the left, and the
columns of a matrix are the images of the basis vectors. Evaluation at γ is the functional
γ: Λ → Z , γ(aγˆ +buˆ+cwˆ +dδˆ) = a, kerγ = ⟨uˆ,wˆ,δˆ⟩.

1 2 1 2
a conjugacy invariant. An element h ∈ ∆ of finite order is conjugate into a free factor, so all its powers have
finite order and cannot be parabolic; hence h has infinite order and is conjugate to a cyclically reduced

0 0

3.6 The family J → B ◦ and its marking
Convention 3.17. Paths are composed left to right: if c(1) = c′(0) then cc′ means “first c, then c′”. With
these conventions the map of §2 sending the homotopy class of π◦c, for a path c from the base point z to
gz in h◦, to g ∈ ∆ is a group homomorphism π (B◦,π(z)) → ∆ .

j

−1,

of B, and then the product of the meridians around p ,p ,p , taken in this order and suitably based, is

5.2 Order, fixed points, freeness
Lemma 5.5. (i) Let A ∈ GL(Λ) have finite order, b ∈ Λ⊗Q , and let α(x) = Ax+b be the induced
self-map of (Λ⊗R)/ Λ . Then α has a fixed point if and only if φ(b) ∈ Z for every φ ∈ V = Hom(Λ , Z)
with φ◦ A = φ. (ii) VT 1 = VT 1 2 = ⟨γ,ψ 1 ⟩ with ψ 1 := 2u+w+3δ, and VT2 = VT 2 2 = VT 2 3 = ⟨γ,ψ 2 ⟩ with
ψ := u+w+2δ.
2
Proof. (i) Put Λ R := Λ⊗R , W := {φ ∈ V : φ◦A = φ}. A fixed point exists iff b ∈ (A−I)Λ R+Λ , which
forces φ(b) ∈ Z for φ ∈ W. Conversely let N be the order of A and p := 1 ∑ Ai, a rational projector
N i<N
with image U = ker(A− I)R and kernel U′ = im(A− I)R, so Λ R = U⊕U′. Every φ ∈ WR := W ⊗R
kills U′ and dimWR = dimU, so the pairing U×WR → R is perfect. The group p(Λ) ⊂ U is a lattice (it
spans U and lies in 1Λ∩U) and φ(pλ) = φ(λ) for φ ∈ WR; hence the dual lattice of p(Λ) is W and, by
N
biduality, p(Λ) = {u ∈ U : φ(u) ∈ Z ∀φ ∈ W}. If φ(b) ∈ Z for all φ ∈ W, then φ(pb) = φ(b) ∈ Z , so
pb = pλ with λ ∈ Λ and b−λ ∈ U′.
(ii) A direct computation with the matrices of §2 gives, for x = aγ+bu+cw+dδ,

The integral homology of X is computed twice on the following pages, by two independent routes: first
by the collapse/Mayer–Vietoris computation — the collapse of a general fibre onto the central fibre W
(Proposition 7.2, proved in §7.2), the homology ofW and the specialisation maps (§7.3), and Mayer–Vietoris
for X = N′ ∪X◦ (§7.6), culminating in Theorem 7.22; and secondly by the Leray computation of §7.7 (“A
0

of X from E 2 a,b = Ha(B,Rbf ∗ Z). Neither computation uses the other: the Leray route takes as input

8 Recognition: X is diffeomorphic to S6.
Throughout this section X denotes the compact complex threefold constructed in §6, regarded as a closed
smooth 6-manifold and oriented by its complex structure.
Theorem 8.1. X is diffeomorphic to S6.
Lemma 8.2. Let M be a closed smooth simply connected n-manifold, n ≥ 2, with H(cid:101)∗ (M; Z) ∼ = H(cid:101)∗ (Sn; Z).
Then M is homotopy equivalent to Sn.
Proof. By the Hurewicz theorem [Hat02, Thm. 4.32], applied inductively in degrees 2,...,n, one gets
π (M) = 0 for i ≤ n−1 and an isomorphism π (M) −→ ∼ H (M; Z) ∼ = Z . Let φ: Sn → M represent a class
i n n
mapping to a generator. By naturality of the Hurewicz map φ∗ is an isomorphism on H
n
, hence (the
remaining reduced groups vanishing) on all integral homology. Both spaces are simply connected and
of the homotopy type of CW complexes: this is clear for Sn, and for the closed topological manifold M it
is [Hat02, Cor. A.12]. Hence φ is a homotopy equivalence by the homology Whitehead theorem [Hat02,
Cor. 4.33].
63
=== PAGE 64 ===
Proof of Theorem 8.1. By Theorem 6.2, X is a compact complex manifold of dimension 3, so its underlying
smooth manifold is closed of real dimension 6; by Theorem 7.17 and Corollary 7.29 (equivalently The-
orem 7.22), π 1 (X) = 1 and H ∗ (X; Z) ∼ = H ∗ (S6; Z). Lemma 8.2 with n = 6 shows that X is a homotopy
6-sphere. By Smale’s generalised Poincaré theorem in dimensions ≥ 5 [Sma61, Thm. A], X is homeomor-
phictoS6 andisatwistedsphere. Bytheh-cobordismtheorem[Mil65],forn ≥ 5theh-cobordismclassesof
oriented homotopy n-spheres coincide with their oriented diffeomorphism classes, and by Kervaire–Milnor
[KM63, Lem. 4.5, Thm. 1.1] these classes form a finite abelian group Θ under connected sum, with Θ = 0
n 6
[KM63, §7]. Hence X is orientation-preservingly diffeomorphic to S6 with one of its two orientations; as S6
carries an orientation-reversing self-diffeomorphism (a reflection of R7), X is diffeomorphic to S6, and the
diffeomorphism may be taken orientation-preserving for the complex orientation.
Remark 8.3. The proof uses only π 1 (X) = 1 (Theorem 7.17) and H ∗ (X; Z) ∼ = H ∗ (S6; Z) (Corollary 7.29
or Theorem 7.22); H ∗ (X; Z) is computed twice in §7: once by the Mayer–Vietoris route, which uses the
retraction and collapse of §7.2, and once by the Leray route of §7.7 with Appendix B, which together with
Theorem 7.17 and Corollary 4.8 does not use §7.2; the recognition of X as S6 therefore does not depend on
Proposition 7.2.
Remark 8.4. It is the diffeomorphism, not merely the homeomorphism of [Sma61], that is needed for the
statement of §1: transporting the complex structure of X through any diffeomorphism X → S6 yields an
integrable almost complex structure on the standard smooth S6 whose complex manifold is biholomorphic
to X.

Proposition 9.7. a(X) = 1, M(X) = f∗M(B), and f is the algebraic reduction of X.
Proof. Let g ∈ M(X) with polar divisor |P|. By Proposition 9.5 the set π(C) ⊂ B◦ is countable, and only
finitely many fibres are contained in |P|; since B is uncountable, there are uncountably many b ∈ B◦ with
a(F ) = 0 and F ̸⊂ |P|.

By Lemma 9.6, g ∈ f∗M(B). Therefore
M(X) = f ∗M(B) ∼ = C(t),
the pullback f∗ being injective because f is surjective; in particular a(X) = trdeg C(t) = 1. Finally, f is a
C
surjective holomorphic map onto the projective curve B with connected fibres and f∗M(B) = M(X), i.e.
f is the algebraic reduction of X.

b b

non-constant meromorphic function.
Proof. Immediate from Proposition 9.5: the exceptional set is Σ∪π(C), a countable subset of the curve B,
and proper analytic subsets of B are exactly the finite sets.

Remark 9.9 (second proof that a(X) = 1). We record the route through [CDP20]. It yields a(X) = 1, and
hence also M(X) = f∗M(B): indeed M(X) then has transcendence degree 1 over C , so it is algebraic
over the subfield f∗M(B), which is algebraically closed in M(X) because f is proper and surjective
with connected fibres onto the normal curve B [Ue75, §3]; whence M(X) = f∗M(B). It does not yield
Corollary 9.8, and it uses the topological computations of §7 and §9.4.

9.6 The remaining Hodge numbers
Remark 9.21 (What is proved, and the expected values). Combining Proposition 9.13, Proposition 9.20,
χ(Ω1) = −1, χ(Ω2) = 1 (§9.4) and Serre duality hp,q = h3−p,3−q, the Hodge numbers of X are
X X
h0,0 = h3,3 = h0,1 = h3,2 = 1, hp,0 = 0 (p ≥ 1), h0,q = 0 (q ≥ 2),
hence also h3,q = 0 for q ≤ 1 and hp,3 = 0 for p ≤ 2; and
χ(Ω1) = h1,0−h1,1+h1,2−h1,3 = −h1,1+h1,2 = −1,
X
χ(Ω2) = h2,0−h2,1+h2,2−h2,3 = −h2,1+h2,2 = 1,
X
the two relations being equivalent under Serre duality. Thus every Hodge number of X is determined by
the single integer
h1,2 = h2,1 ≥ 0, via h1,1 = h2,2 = h1,2+1.
The remaining Hodge numbers are determined by h1,2, which we do not compute; the expected value is
h1,2 = h2,1 = 1, whence h1,1 = h2,2 = 2.
(Equivalently: dimCE
1
of the Frölicher spectral sequence equals 6+4h1,2, expected 10.) A proof of
h1,2 = 1 would require the direct images Rqf ∗ Ω1 X (logD), D := W +S 1 +S 2 , equivalently those of
E := Ω1(logD)/f∗Ω1(log Σ), at the three singular fibres, where Ω1 has torsion along W∪S ∪S and
X B X/B 1 2
the invariant-theoretic description on the covers J′ and on Y must be matched with the residue sequences.
j F
Corollary 9.22. The Frölicher spectral sequence of X does not degenerate at E . Moreover ∂: H0,1 → H1,1
1
is injective, so h1,1 ≥ 1.
Proof. By Proposition 9.13, h0,1 = 1, and h1,0 = 0 by Proposition 9.20. If the spectral sequence degenerated
at E , then b (X) = h1,0+h0,1 = 1. But b (X) = 0: indeed X is simply connected by the van Kampen
1 1 1
computation of §7 (a computation with the fundamental groups of the four pieces J,N ,N ,N and of their
1 2 0
overlaps). This contradiction proves the first assertion.
For the second, the generator of H0,1 must die in the spectral sequence, since E∞ 0,1 is a subquotient
of H1(X; C) = 0. No differential enters E0,1 (the groups E −r,r vanish). The differentials leaving it are
r r
d = ∂: H0,1 → H1,1; d : E0,1 → E2,0, whose target is a subquotient of H2,0 = 0 (Proposition 9.20); and d
1 2 2 2 r
for r ≥ 3, with target Er,2−r = 0 since 2−r < 0. Hence the class must be killed by d , i.e. ∂ is injective on
r 1

The scope of the discussion below is the following. What is located at each place is a step of a printed
proof; where the conclusion of the statement itself does not fail for X, the statement may well survive by
other arguments, and this applies in particular to [CDP20, Proposition 5.6] under hypotheses different
from those of X, whose conclusion is in any case true for X (Proposition 10.8). Where the conclusion of a

instead is described in §10.6.

Lemma 10.7. Let 1 → K → G → Q → 1 be a split exact sequence of groups. Then Gab ∼ = Qab⊕(Kab) , the
Q

Proof. Chooseasplitting, G = K ⋊ Q,andlet K′ ⊂ K begeneratedby [K,K] andall k−1qkq−1 (k ∈ K, q ∈ Q).
Then K′ is normal in G: it is normalised by K since it contains [K,K], and by Q since q′[K,K]q′−1 = [K,K]
and q′(k−1qkq−1)q′−1 = k −1q k q −1 with k = q′kq′−1, q = q′qq′−1. Moreover K/K′ = (Kab) with trivial
1 1 1 1 1 1 Q
Q-action, so G/K′ ∼ = (Kab) ×Q; as K′ ⊂ [G,G], Gab = (G/K′)ab ∼ = (Kab) ⊕Qab.

The hypotheses under which this is asserted are the standing hypotheses of [CDP20, §5], which
we paraphrase: X is a 3-dimensional compact complex manifold with b (X) = 0 (in the application,
2
H1(X, Z) = H2(X, Z) = 0) and a(X) = 1 whose algebraic reduction f: X → C is holomorphic, the general
fibre X c beingacomplex2-torusoraKodairasurface,and R1f ∗ O X islocallyfree([CDP20,Proposition5.1]).
The proof of Corollary 5.2 reads “Suppose first that F is a Kodaira surface or a torus. Then the assertion
is Theorem 3.1 in [CDP98]; the proof works since we now know that R1f ∗ (O X ) is locally free”. Our X
satisfies all of these hypotheses, by (X1), (X2) and (X4). The local freeness of R1f ∗ O X demanded here does
91
=== PAGE 92 ===
hold for X — it is local freeness of R1f ∗ O X with base change (Proposition 9.13), whose statement gives
local freeness together with a splitting; base change then follows by descending induction on q in the
cohomology-and-base-change theorem for the flat proper morphism f, all Rqf ∗ O X being locally free [BaSt,
Ch. III, §3] — although the printed proof of [CDP20, Proposition 5.1] passes through a step which does not
apply to X.5
At X, however, more than a step fails: the conclusion itself fails for the X constructed here. By (X2),
H1(X,O ) ∼ = C (Theorem 9.1), while H1(F,O ) ∼ = C2 for a smooth fibre F, a complex 2-torus by (X4);
X F
hence the restriction
H1(X,O ) −→ H1(F,O )
X F
is not surjective, so the conclusion of [CDP98, Theorem 3.1]=[CDP20, Corollary 5.2, p. 688] fails for
the X constructed here. At X the statement therefore cannot be restored by repairing the monodromy
line, nor by any other argument. Consistently with this, for X the Leray spectral sequence E p,q =
2
Hp(P1,Rqf ∗ Q) ⇒ Hp+q(X; Q) forces d 2 : E 2 0,1 → E 2 2,0 = H2(P1; Q) ∼ = Q to be injective (H1(X; Q) = 0) and
surjective (H2(X; Q) = 0), so H0(P1,R1f ∗ Q) ∼ = Q , in agreement with t′ = 1 and not with t = 4.
The consequences for [CDP20, §5] are immediate. The statement at issue is:
5.5. Proposition (p. 688). “R1f ∗ (O X ) ≃ O(b 1 )⊕O(b 2 ) with b j ≥ 0.”
(The splitting into line bundles is Grothendieck’s on C ∼ = P1, R1f ∗ O X being locally free of rank 2
under the standing hypotheses; what Proposition 5.5 adds is the positivity b ≥ 0.) Its proof reads “We
j
observe that R1f ∗ (O X ) is generically spanned by Corollary 5.2, since H1(X,O X ) = H0(C,R1f ∗ (O X )).
Hence b ≥ 0”. The conclusion of [CDP20, Proposition 5.5, p. 688] fails for the X constructed here:
j
R1f ∗ O X ∼ = O P1 ⊕O P1 (−1) by (X2) (§9), a summand of negative degree; so that conclusion (and likewise
that of [CDP98, Remark 3.8(1), p. 88], Remark 5.8(1) of the arXiv text) fails for the X constructed here, and
is not merely unproved there. Finally, [CDP20, Proposition 5.6, p. 689] uses the positivity of the summands
b to produce a line bundle L ∈ Pic(X) whose restriction L| to every fibre is non-torsion — the “key”
j S
quoted in [CDP20, Proposition 2.4, p. 680], and the input which [CDP20, §5] is to deliver to [CDP20, §7].
That argument is unconditionally unavailable at X: the hypothesis b ≥ 0 on which it rests fails at X.
j
Its conclusion, however, is true for X, by a countability argument resting on Proposition 9.13. Theo-
rem 10.5(b) shows that for each individual fibre the set of z ∈ C ∼ = Pic(X) with L torsion on that fibre is
z

single L non-torsion on all fibres simultaneously, the fibres forming an uncountable family and a union of
uncountably many countable sets needing not be proper. The periods of f supply the missing uniformity.

B.7 Saturation and invariants
• q = 0: trivial. q = 4: E∞ 4,0 = H4(F) (the other pieces E∞ 3,1,E∞ 2,2 vanish), so sp4 is an isomorphism.
• q = 1: H1(F)/imsp1 = E∞ 0,1 = H0(W;V) ∼ = Z2 is free (α = 0), so imsp1 is saturated of rank 2.
• q = 2: H2(F)/F2 has graded pieces E∞ 1,1 = H1(W;V) ∼ = Z (as β = 0) and E∞ 0,2 = ker (cid:0) d

) are monodromy invariant), imspq is

rkHq(F)T0 = rk(∧qV)T0 = (1,2,4,2,1)
(Lemma A.1(7)). A saturated subgroup of the (saturated) invariant subgroup, of the same rank, is the
whole of it. This proves Theorem B.1.
B.8 Scope
Remark B.4. Theorem B.1 supplies the injectivity of spq and the identification of its image with the T -
0
invariants, which is what §§7.4–7.7 use of Proposition 7.12; the identification of spq with c∗ for the collapse
map c, the generators of H (W), and the normalisations sp2(T∨) = γ∧u, sp2(C¯∨) of Proposition 7.12
2 B k
are not re-proved here and continue to rest on §7.2 and Proposition 7.12. The ancillary facts used above
— the toric local models of Theorem 4.5, the hexagon combinatorics of Proposition 4.6, the flat model of
F = Y/ Γ with its T -action (Proposition 4.7), and Lemma A.1 — do not involve §7.2; the ranks (1,2,4,2,1)
t t f
of H•(W; Z) are computed in §B.4 from the resolution (B.2) and agree with, but are not taken from,
Proposition 7.11.
